        "Algorithm Steps:",
        "1. Create a helper function that takes the `current_string`.",
        "2. Base Case: If the length of `current_string` equals `n`, add it to a `results` list and stop (return).",

        "4. Make Choice 2: Append '1' to the string and call the helper function again.",
        "5. Return the `results` list.",
        "",
        "Instructions:",
        "1. Create `binary_strings(n)`.",
        "2. Write the recursive helper function inside it.",
        "3. Watch the analyzer assign an O(2^n) Time Complexity, because every step creates 2 new branches.",
        "",
        "Sample Output:",
        "binary_strings(2) â†’ ['00', '01', '10', '11']"

      "task": [
        "To find all possible subsets of a list, you make a simple choice for every single item: you either 'take it' or you 'skip it'.",
        "",
        "Algorithm Steps:",
        "1. Create a helper function that tracks the `index` and the `current_path`.",
        "2. Base Case: If `index` reaches the end of the array, save a copy of `current_path` to your results and return.",
        "3. Choice 1 (Take): Append the item at `arr[index]` to the path, recurse to `index + 1`, then pop the item off (this is the rollback!).",
        "4. Choice 2 (Skip): Recurse to `index + 1` without adding the item.",
        "",
        "Instructions:",
        "1. Create `generate_subsets(arr)`.",
        "2. Write the take/skip recursive logic and remember the `.pop()` rollback step.",
        "",
        "Sample Output:",
        "generate_subsets([1, 2]) â†’ [[1, 2], [1], [2], []]"

      "title": "Permutations (Order Matters)",
      "task": [
        "Permutations are all the possible ways to order a list. Because you can't reuse numbers you've already picked, you need a way to track what is currently in your path.",
        "",
        "Algorithm Steps:",
        "1. Create a helper function with `current_path`.",
        "2. Base Case: If the path length equals the array length, save a copy of the path.",
        "3. Loop through every number in the array.",
        "4. If the number is already in `current_path`, skip it (`continue`).",
        "5. Otherwise, append the number, call the helper function, and then `.pop()` the number (rollback) so you can try the next one.",
        "",
        "Instructions:",
        "1. Create `get_permutations(arr)`.",
        "2. Write the loop and rollback logic.",
        "3. Notice the Time Complexity is O(n!) because the choices decrease by 1 each step (n * n-1 * n-2...).",
        "",
        "Sample Output:",
        "get_permutations([1, 2]) â†’ [[1, 2], [2, 1]]"

      "code": "def knapsack_01(weights, values, capacity):\n    n = len(weights)\n    dp = [[0]*(capacity+1) for _ in range(n+1)]  # (n+1) \u00d7 (capacity+1) table\n    for i in range(1, n+1):\n        for w in range(capacity+1):\n            if weights[i-1] <= w:\n                dp[i][w] = max(dp[i-1][w],\n                               values[i-1] + dp[i-1][w-weights[i-1]])\n            else:\n                dp[i][w] = dp[i-1][w]\n    return dp[n][capacity]",
      "question": "What are the time and space complexities of this 0/1 Knapsack DP solution?",
      "options": [
        "Time: O(n\u00b2), Space: O(n)",

        "Time: O(n log n), Space: O(capacity)"

      "title": "Input Space vs Auxiliary Space",
      "content": "Input space is the memory already required to store the input itself. For example, the list [1, 2, 3, 4, 5] occupies memory before the algorithm begins doing extra work.\n\nAuxiliary space is additional memory created by the algorithm. Examples include temporary arrays, helper data structures, and recursion stack frames. Engineers often focus on auxiliary space because it represents extra memory overhead introduced by the implementation."
    },
    {
      "id": "constant-space",

          "code": "def iterative_binary_search(values, target):\n    low = 0\n    high = len(values) - 1\n\n    while low <= high:\n        mid = (low + high) // 2\n        if values[mid] == target:\n            return mid\n        if values[mid] < target:\n            low = mid + 1\n        else:\n            high = mid - 1\n\n    return -1\n\nprint(iterative_binary_search([2, 4, 8, 16, 32], 16))"

This strategy is called brute force.\n\nA brute force algorithm does not begin with shortcuts or advanced optimization. It uses direct, systematic checking. The algorithm explores candidate answers, tests whether each candidate satisfies the requirement, and stops only when the answer is found or every possibility has been exhausted.\n\nBrute force is important because it is often the first correct version of a solution. Many advanced algorithms are easier to understand when they are viewed as optimizations of a simpler brute force approach."

      "content": "The core idea of brute force is systematic testing. In password-like problems, it may mean trying every possible code.\n\nBecause every possibility is checked, brute force can guarantee correctness if the implementation is accurate and the search space is finite."

    },
    {
      "id": "step-by-step",

None of those values match.\n\nWhen the algorithm compares 11 with 11, the target is found and the index is returned. Because the target appears before the end, the algorithm can stop early.\n\nIf the program searches for a value that does not exist, every element must be checked. The algorithm reaches the end and returns -1 to communicate that the target was not found."

      "title": "What is Bubble Sort?",
      "content": "Bubble Sort is a simple comparison-based sorting algorithm. It repeatedly compares adjacent elements and swaps them when they are in the wrong order.\n\nDuring each pass, larger values gradually move toward the end of the array, similar to bubbles rising upward in water. This is why the algorithm is called Bubble Sort."
    },
    {
      "id": "core-idea",
      "type": "concept",
      "title": "Core Idea of Bubble Sort",
      "content": "Bubble Sort works through repeated passes. During each pass, adjacent elements are compared. If the left value is greater than the right value in ascending order, the two values are swapped.\n\nAfter one full pass, the largest unsorted value is usually in its correct final position. The algorithm then repeats the process for the remaining unsorted portion of the array."
    },
    {
      "id": "walkthrough",
      "type": "deep_dive",
      "title": "Step-by-Step Example",
      "content": "Suppose the algorithm sorts [5, 2, 8, 1] in ascending order.\n\nDuring the first pass, 5 and 2 are compared. Since 5 is greater than 2, they are swapped, producing [2, 5, 8, 1]. Next, 5 and 8 are compared and left unchanged. Then 8 and 1 are compared and swapped, producing [2, 5, 1, 8]. The largest value, 8, has reached the end.\n\nThe next pass starts again from the beginning. Values 2 and 5 are already in order, but 5 and 1 must be swapped, producing [2, 1, 5, 8]. A final pass swaps 2 and 1, producing [1, 2, 5, 8]."
    },
    {
      "id": "implementation",
      "type": "concept",
      "title": "Python Implementation",
      "content": "Bubble Sort is usually implemented with nested loops. The outer loop controls repeated passes. The inner loop compares adjacent pairs and performs swaps.",
      "codeSnippets": [
        {
          "title": "Bubble Sort in ascending order",
          "language": "python",
          "code": "def bubble_sort_asc(arr):\n    result = arr[:]\n\n    for pass_index in range(len(result)):\n        for i in range(0, len(result) - pass_index - 1):\n            if result[i] > result[i + 1]:\n                result[i], result[i + 1] = result[i + 1], result[i]\n\n    return result\n\nprint(bubble_sort_asc([5, 2, 8, 1]))"
        },
        {
          "title": "Bubble Sort in descending order",
          "language": "python",
          "code": "def bubble_sort_desc(arr):\n    result = arr[:]\n\n    for pass_index in range(len(result)):\n        for i in range(0, len(result) - pass_index - 1):\n            if result[i] < result[i + 1]:\n                result[i], result[i + 1] = result[i + 1], result[i]\n\n    return result\n\nprint(bubble_sort_desc([1, 2, 3]))"
        },
        {
          "title": "Optimized Bubble Sort stops when no swaps occur",
          "language": "python",
          "code": "def optimized_bubble_sort(arr):\n    result = arr[:]\n\n    for pass_index in range(len(result)):\n        swapped = False\n\n        for i in range(0, len(result) - pass_index - 1):\n            if result[i] > result[i + 1]:\n                result[i], result[i + 1] = result[i + 1], result[i]\n                swapped = True\n\n        if not swapped:\n            break\n\n    return result\n\nprint(optimized_bubble_sort([1, 2, 3, 4]))"
        }
      ]

      "title": "Introduction",
      "content": "Some computational problems become difficult to solve efficiently when treated as one huge task. Instead of attacking the entire problem directly, programmers often use a strategy called Divide and Conquer.\n\nDivide and Conquer works by breaking a large problem into smaller subproblems, solving those smaller problems independently, and then combining their solutions into the final answer. Rather than solving one massive task all at once, the algorithm repeatedly handles simpler pieces.\n\nThis strategy is one of the most important ideas in computer science because many efficient searching, sorting, graphics, AI, and parallel-processing algorithms rely on recursive problem reduction."
    },
    {
      "id": "why-divide-and-conquer-matters",
      "type": "concept",
      "title": "Why Divide and Conquer Matters",
      "content": "Large-scale systems constantly process massive datasets. Solving those problems directly can become inefficient or difficult to manage. Divide and Conquer improves scalability by aggressively reducing problem size and focusing on smaller manageable tasks.\n\nInstead of handling everything at once, the algorithm decomposes the workload into smaller independent sections. These smaller sections are often easier to solve, easier to reason about, and sometimes even possible to execute in parallel.",
      "bullets": [
        "Smaller subproblems are easier to manage.",
        "Recursive solutions become simpler to design.",
        "Parallel execution becomes possible.",
        "Efficient algorithms often emerge from recursive decomposition."

    },
    {
      "id": "core-idea",
      "type": "concept",
      "title": "Core Idea of Divide and Conquer",
      "content": "Divide and Conquer follows three major phases: divide, conquer, and combine.\n\nDuring the divide phase, the original problem is broken into smaller subproblems. During the conquer phase, those smaller subproblems are solved independently, often through recursion. During the combine phase, the partial solutions are merged together to produce the final result.\n\nThe strategy repeatedly reduces the workload until the remaining subproblem becomes simple enough to solve directly."

          "title": "Iterative Binary Search",
          "language": "python",
          "code": "def binary_search(arr, target):\n    low = 0\n    high = len(arr) - 1\n\n    while low <= high:\n        mid = (low + high) // 2\n\n        if arr[mid] == target:\n            return mid\n        elif target > arr[mid]:\n            low = mid + 1\n        else:\n            high = mid - 1\n\n    return -1\n\nprint(binary_search([1, 3, 5, 7, 9, 11, 15], 11))"

      "content": "Quick Sort repeatedly partitions smaller subarrays.\n\nSuppose the array begins as [8, 3, 5, 1, 7]. After partitioning around pivot 7, the array becomes [3, 5, 1] 7 [8].\n\nThe algorithm recursively partitions the left side again using another pivot. Each recursive step reduces disorder further until the array becomes fully sorted.\n\nThis recursive partitioning is the core Divide and Conquer behavior behind Quick Sort.",
      "chart": {
        "title": "Quick Sort recursive reduction",
        "description": "Quick Sort repeatedly partitions arrays into smaller sections, often producing logarithmic recursive depth for balanced partitions.",

      "id": "why-quick-sort-fast",
      "type": "concept",
      "title": "Why Quick Sort Is Fast",
      "content": "Quick Sort becomes efficient because partitioning rapidly reduces problem size.\n\nBalanced partitions create recursive trees similar to repeatedly dividing n into n/2, then n/4, then n/8.\n\nThis recursive shrinking often produces O(n log n) average-case complexity, which scales efficiently for large datasets."
    },
    {
      "id": "implementation",
      "type": "concept",
      "title": "Python Implementation",
      "content": "Quick Sort implementations usually rely on recursive partitioning and pivot-based decomposition.",
      "codeSnippets": [
        {
          "title": "Quick Sort implementation",
          "language": "python",
          "code": "def quick_sort(arr):\n    if len(arr) <= 1:\n        return arr\n\n    pivot = arr[-1]\n\n    left = []\n    right = []\n\n    for value in arr[:-1]:\n        if value < pivot:\n            left.append(value)\n        else:\n            right.append(value)\n\n    return quick_sort(left) + [pivot] + quick_sort(right)\n\nprint(quick_sort([8, 3, 5, 1, 7]))"
        },
        {
          "title": "Partition demonstration",

          "code": "def partition_demo(arr, pivot):\n    left = []\n    right = []\n\n    for value in arr:\n        if value < pivot:\n            left.append(value)\n        elif value > pivot:\n            right.append(value)\n\n    return left, pivot, right\n\nprint(partition_demo([8, 3, 5, 1], 5))"
        },

    },
    {
      "id": "core-greedy-idea",
      "type": "concept",
      "title": "Core Greedy Idea",
      "content": "The greedy strategy is simple: always select the activity that finishes earliest.\n\nAt first, this may seem unusual. Choosing the earliest finishing activity maximizes the remaining time available for future activities.\n\nThis increases the chance of selecting additional compatible activities later."
    },
    {
      "id": "why-earliest-finishing-works",
      "type": "deep_dive",
      "title": "Why Earliest Finishing Works",
      "content": "Suppose we select an activity that finishes very late, such as (1, 10). This blocks many future activities.\n\nHowever, if we instead choose an activity such as (1, 3), more scheduling space remains afterward.\n\nThe greedy strategy succeeds because early finishing preserves maximum scheduling flexibility.\n\nThis is the key intuition behind the algorithm."
    },
    {
      "id": "step-by-step-example",
      "type": "deep_dive",
      "title": "Step-by-Step Execution Example",

      "content": "Dynamic Programming (DP) is an algorithmic technique used to solve problems by reusing previously computed results.\n\nInstead of solving the same subproblem repeatedly, Dynamic Programming stores solutions and reuses them whenever needed.\n\nThis dramatically improves efficiency for problems involving repeated recursive computation, overlapping subproblems, and optimization tasks.\n\nDynamic Programming is one of the most powerful strategies in computer science and appears in AI systems, optimization engines, pathfinding algorithms, financial systems, and game development."
    },

      "id": "why-dp-matters",

However, for large inputs, execution time can explode dramatically.\n\nDynamic Programming solves this by remembering already-computed answers and reusing them whenever needed.",

          "code": "def fib(n):\n    if n <= 1:\n        return n\n\n    prev2 = 0\n    prev1 = 1\n\n    for i in range(2, n + 1):\n        current = prev1 + prev2\n        prev2 = prev1\n        prev1 = current\n\n    return prev1\n\nprint(fib(10))"
        }
      ]
